The complete table in Appendix <a href="#app:cochain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:cochain">10</a> defines an $I$-bimodule map $$p:\mathfrak r\otimes_I\mathfrak r\otimes_I\mathfrak r\longrightarrow T.$$ Every unlisted value is zero. The following records exactly the properties of this finite table that the proof needs.

<a id="stmt-coc-data"></a>
<div id="coc:data" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7**. *The cochain $p$ has weight $-1$ and is a Hochschild cocycle. Its associated class evaluated on $s$ is nonzero in $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^3(s,s)$.*

<a id="coc:boundary"></a>
*For any $H\in k^\times$, let $h=h_H$ be the automorphism of $T$ fixing $C$ and multiplying $L$ by $H$. On the radical basis define $$z_H(a)=
 \begin{cases}
 Hqa,&a\in\{u,v,t,E,X,Y,Z,U,V\},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}$$ and extend linearly. For all $a,b\in\mathfrak r$ one has $$\sum_{w\in\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}}
      p(a,b,h(w))w^*
  =a z_H(b)+z_H(ab)+z_H(a)h^{-1}(b),
\label{coc:boundary}$$ where $\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}$ is the radical basis.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-coc-data"><summary>Proof of stmt-coc-data</summary>

<a id="coc:cycle"></a>
*Proof.* The endpoint, weight, cocycle and boundary identities are verified from the complete finite data in Appendix <a href="#app:cochain" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="app:cochain">10</a>. We prove the nonzero evaluation explicitly. In the bar complex $s^{\mathrm r}\otimes_T P\otimes_T s$, consider $$\zeta=q^2[t|x|J]+[t|y|J].
\label{coc:cycle}$$ Both words have endpoint $f$ on each side. All outer radical actions vanish on the endpoint simples. The multiplication rules, including the dual actions, give $$tx=j,\qquad ty=q^2j,\qquad xJ=T,\qquad yJ=q^2T.$$ Consequently $$d\zeta=q^2([j|J]+[t|T])+q^2[j|J]+q^2[t|T]=0.$$ The table gives $p(t,x,J)=0$ and $p(t,y,J)=q^3f$, so its evaluation on $\zeta$ is $q^3\ne0$. Every $T$-linear map from $P\otimes_T s$ to $s$ factors through the left simple quotient and hence pairs with this two-sided-simple bar complex. A coboundary pairs to zero with its cycles. Thus the evaluated class is nonzero. ◻

</details>

The evaluation of a Hochschild class on modules is the characteristic action; see (Lowen 2008, sec. 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[Lowen2008](#ref-Lowen2008)]. We use the same letter $p$ for its comparison map $P\to P[3]$: for $n\ge3$ apply the cochain to the last three bars and multiply its output into the right coefficient, <a id="coc:comparison"></a>$$a_0[a_1|\cdots|a_n]a_{n+1}\longmapsto
 a_0[a_1|\cdots|a_{n-3}]
 p(a_{n-2},a_{n-1},a_n)a_{n+1};
\label{coc:comparison}$$ the map is zero for $n<3$. This is a chain map: strict-prefix multiplications cancel, and the remaining equation on the last four bars is precisely the Hochschild cocycle equation. At the bottom index the target differential is zero. Composing its evaluation on $s$ with the augmentation represents the class in Lemma <a href="#coc:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="coc:data">7</a>.

We have now obtained the finite algebra and the specific degree-three class. The next step is to compute all self-extension degrees of $s$, so that this nonzero class can be identified as a polynomial generator.

<a id="res:section"></a>
### A simple module with polynomial self-extensions

The cocycle constructed above will control the two cones in our example. We now determine all self-extensions of the simple on which it acts. The key step is a calculation over the smaller algebra $C$: its simple $s$ has no positive self-extensions, but $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_C^2(s,C)$ is a simple module. Passing to the trivial extension $T=C\ltimes DC$ turns this exceptional class into a polynomial generator of degree three.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### The resolution over $C$

Let $s^{\mathrm r}$ denote the one-dimensional right $f$-simple. For every integer $i\geq0$, set $$\ell_i=x+q^i y.$$ The transcendence of $q$ implies $q\ne0$ and $1+q^m\ne0$ for every positive integer $m$. We will use these conditions for arbitrarily large indices.

<a id="stmt-res-base"></a>
<div id="res:base" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8**. *A projective resolution $R\to s$ over $C$ has terms $$R_0=Cf,\qquad R_i=Ce\quad(i\geq1),$$ and differentials given by right multiplication: $$\cdots\longrightarrow Ce
 \xrightarrow{\,\cdot\ell_1\,}Ce
 \xrightarrow{\,\cdot\ell_0\,}Ce
 \xrightarrow{\,\cdot u\,}Cf\longrightarrow s\longrightarrow0.$$ More precisely, $d_{i+2}$ is right multiplication by $\ell_i$ for every $i\geq0$. For every integer $i\geq0$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_C^i(s,s)=
 \begin{cases}k&i=0,\\0&i>0,\end{cases}
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_C^i(s,C)=
 \begin{cases}s^{\mathrm r}&i=2,\\0&i\ne2.\end{cases}$$ The second identification is an identification of right $C$-modules.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-base"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-base</summary>

*Proof.* The left ideals $Ce$ and $Cf$ have respective bases $$(e,x,y,z,t,j),\qquad(f,u,v,n).$$ The multiplication table gives the following formulas, valid for every $i\geq0$: $$\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
 a&e&x&y&z&t&j\\ \hline
 au&u&v&v&0&(1+q)n&0\\
 a\ell_i&\ell_i&q^{i+1}z&z&0&(1+q^{i+2})j&0.
\end{array}$$ Consequently, right multiplication by $u$ has image $\langle u,v,n\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(Cf)$ and kernel $\langle\ell_0,z,j\rangle$. Right multiplication by $\ell_i$ has image $\langle\ell_i,z,j\rangle$ and kernel $\langle\ell_{i+1},z,j\rangle$. In the latter calculation, a kernel vector has zero $e$- and $t$-coefficients, while its $x,y$ coefficients satisfy $a_y=q^{i+1}a_x$. These identities prove exactness at every index. Since $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Ce,s)=es=0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Cf,s)=fs=k$, they also give the asserted self-extension groups over $C$.

To compute $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_C^*(s,C)$, identify $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Ce,C)=eC$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(Cf,C)=fC$ by evaluation at the idempotent. Precomposition with right multiplication becomes left multiplication. Thus the Hom complex is $$fC\xrightarrow{\,u\cdot\,}eC
 \xrightarrow{\,\ell_0\cdot\,}eC
 \xrightarrow{\,\ell_1\cdot\,}eC\longrightarrow\cdots.$$ Left multiplication by $u$ sends the basis $(f,t,j,n)$ of $fC$ to $(u,y+qx,z,v)$. These four vectors are independent. On the basis $(e,x,y,z,u,v)$ of $eC$, left multiplication by $\ell_i$ is $$\begin{array}{c|cccccc}
 a&e&x&y&z&u&v\\ \hline
 \ell_i a&\ell_i&q^i z&qz&0&(1+q^i)v&0.
\end{array}$$ The index $i=0$ is exceptional: $$\ker(\ell_0\cdot)=\langle qx+y,z,u,v\rangle=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(u\cdot),
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{im}}(\ell_0\cdot)=\langle\ell_0,z\rangle.$$ For every $i\geq1$, the coefficient $1+q^i$ is nonzero, and $$\ker(\ell_i\cdot)=\langle\ell_{i-1},z,v\rangle,
 \qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{im}}(\ell_i\cdot)=\langle\ell_i,z,v\rangle.$$ The cohomology in degrees zero and one therefore vanishes. In degree two it is the line represented by $v$; in every higher degree the displayed kernel equals the preceding image. Finally, $vf=v$, $ve=0$, and the only possibly nonzero right radical product is $vt=qz$, which is a boundary. The surviving line is precisely $s^{\mathrm r}$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The trivial-extension triangle and its product

Write $L=DC$. The duality calculation in Lemma <a href="#res:base" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="res:base">8</a> determines $L\otimes_C^{\mathbf L}s$. The extension $0\to L\to T\to C\to0$ then provides a connecting morphism over $T$. We identify its products explicitly so that the calculation controls every positive degree.

<a id="stmt-res-polynomial"></a>
<div id="res:polynomial" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9**. *For the algebra $T=C\ltimes DC$ and its left $f$-simple $s$, $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^*(s,s)=k[\tau],\qquad |\tau|=3,$$ as a graded algebra, where $\tau=p(s)$ is the evaluation of the cocycle from Lemma <a href="#coc:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="coc:data">7</a>.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-polynomial"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-polynomial</summary>

*Proof.* For a finite projective left $C$-module $V$, evaluation gives a natural isomorphism of left $C$-modules $$DC\otimes_C V\longrightarrow D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(V,C),\qquad
 \lambda\otimes v\longmapsto
 \bigl(\varphi\longmapsto\lambda(\varphi(v))\bigr).$$ Apply this isomorphism termwise to the resolution $R$ of Lemma <a href="#res:base" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="res:base">8</a>. Exactness of vector-space duality shows that $L\otimes_C R$ has homology only in degree two, where it is $D(s^{\mathrm r})=s$. Moreover, $L$ acts by zero on the entire complex, since $L^2=0$. Good truncations therefore give a $T$-linear derived identification $$L\otimes_C R\simeq s[2].$$

<a id="res:triangle"></a>
Regard $0\to L\to T\to C\to0$ as a sequence of $(T,C)$-bimodules. Tensoring it termwise with $R$ gives a short exact sequence of complexes of left $T$-modules, $$0\longrightarrow L\otimes_C R
 \longrightarrow T\otimes_C R
 \longrightarrow R\longrightarrow0,$$ where the last complex is inflated along $T\to C$. Exactness uses projectivity of each left $C$-module $R_i$; it does not require flatness of $L$ or $T$ as right $C$-modules. Put $\mathcal I=T\otimes_C R$. We obtain the derived triangle $$\label{res:triangle}
 s[2]\longrightarrow\mathcal I\longrightarrow s
 \xrightarrow{\,\delta\,}s[3].$$ The middle complex has homology $s$ in degrees zero and two and no other homology. Its terms are finite dimensional, so good truncation makes it an object of the bounded derived category of finite $T$-modules.

Each term of $\mathcal I$ is a finite projective $T$-module. Because the complex is bounded below homologically, it computes derived maps out of it: a map from it to an acyclic complex is nullhomotopic by successive projective lifts starting in its bottom degree. Termwise induction and restriction adjunction thus gives, for every integer $i$, $$B^i:=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{D^b(T\text{-mod})}(\mathcal I,s[i])
 =H^i\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_C(R,s)
 =\begin{cases}k&i=0,\\0&i\ne0.\end{cases}$$

<a id="res:recurrence"></a>
Set $E^i=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{D^b(T\text{-mod})}(s,s[i])$. These are ordinary Ext groups for $i\geq0$ and vanish for $i<0$. Applying $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}(-,s[i])$ to <a href="#res:triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:triangle">[res:triangle]</a> gives the exact segment $$\label{res:recurrence}
 B^{i-1}\longrightarrow E^{i-3}
 \xrightarrow{\,\delta^*\,}E^i\longrightarrow B^i.$$ For $i=1$, the groups $E^{-2}$ and $B^1$ vanish, so $E^1=0$. For every $i\geq2$, both displayed $B$-groups vanish; hence $\delta^*:E^{i-3}\to E^i$ is an isomorphism. This includes $E^2=0$, and at $i=3$ it sends $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_s$ to the nonzero class $\delta$.

More precisely, if $\beta:s\to s[i-3]$, its image under this connecting map is $$\delta^*(\beta)=\beta[3]\circ\delta.$$ Thus <a href="#res:recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="res:recurrence">[res:recurrence]</a> is multiplication by $\delta$ in the Yoneda algebra. Every power $\delta^m$ is nonzero and spans $E^{3m}$, and all other nonnegative degrees vanish. We have proved $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_T^*(s,s)=k[\delta]$ as an algebra.

Lemma <a href="#coc:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="coc:data">7</a> shows that $p(s)$ is nonzero in degree three: the simple-valued cochain evaluates to $q^3\ne0$ on the cycle $q^2[t|x|J]+[t|y|J]$. Hence $p(s)$ is a nonzero scalar multiple of $\delta$ and is itself a polynomial generator. This proves the stated normalization $\tau=p(s)$ without choosing an isomorphism $L\otimes_C R\simeq s[2]$ with a prescribed scalar. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Two independent degree-three generators

We now pass to the symmetric algebra and simple used in the remaining construction: $$A=T\otimes_{k}T,\qquad X=s\otimes_{k}s.$$ The tensor product of the symmetrizing forms makes $A$ symmetric, and $\dim_{k}A=400$. Let $p_1,p_2$ be the external copies of the bimodule class $p$ on the two factors, and write $\tau_i=p_i(X)$ for their evaluations.

<a id="stmt-res-tensor"></a>
<div id="res:tensor" class="lemma">

**Lemma 10**. *The simple left $A$-module $X$ is nonprojective, and $$\bigoplus_{a\geq0}\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,X[a])
 =k[\tau_1,\tau_2],\qquad |\tau_1|=|\tau_2|=3.$$ The multiplication is composition with shifts, and its positive-degree part agrees with the ordinary Ext algebra.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-res-tensor"><summary>Proof of stmt-res-tensor</summary>

*Proof.* Tensor two projective $T$-resolutions of $s$ over $k$. In each total degree the direct sum is finite, so this is a resolution of $X$ by finite projective $A$-modules. The Hom complex is the total tensor product of the two Hom complexes over $T$. Splitting complexes of vector spaces gives the Künneth isomorphism. Tensoring the comparison maps identifies multiplication as well; the external maps commute in characteristic two. Lemma <a href="#res:polynomial" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="res:polynomial">9</a> therefore gives $$\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_A^*(X,X)=k[\tau_1,\tau_2].$$ In particular $X$ has a nonzero positive self-extension and cannot be projective. Its ordinary endomorphism ring is $k$, since $X$ is one dimensional. Its identity cannot factor through a projective module: such a factorization would make $X$ a direct summand of that projective. Thus its stable endomorphism ring is also $k$. Positive stable Hom agrees with Ext over the symmetric algebra $A$, giving the result. ◻

</details>

We have obtained two polynomial classes acting on $X$, together with specific bimodule representatives for them. The next step uses their cones to leave only two stable Hom groups; their degrees will determine the shift in the subsequent fiber construction.

<a id="cone:section"></a>
### Two cones with two surviving Hom groups

<a id="cone:positive-ring"></a>
We now use the two degree-three classes to construct an $A$-bimodule $\mathcal C$, projective on each side, for which the stable maps from $X$ to $\mathcal C\otimes_A X[a]$ survive only in degrees $0$ and $3$. The degree-three group will detect the maps constructed in the next section. Throughout this section, put $$A=T\otimes_{k}T,\qquad X=s\otimes_{k}s,\qquad R=A^e.$$ The algebras $A$ and $R$ are symmetric: their symmetrizing forms are the tensor products of the form on $T$ and, for opposite factors, the same form. Write $$H^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,X[a]).$$ The preceding calculation gives $$H^{\geq0}=k[\tau_1,\tau_2],\qquad |\tau_1|=|\tau_2|=3.
 \label{cone:positive-ring}$$ In degree zero this is also the stable endomorphism space: $X$ is one-dimensional, and its nonzero degree-three self-extension proves that it is nonprojective.

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Negative degrees and multiplication

<a id="cone:duality"></a>
We first record the symmetric stable duality, including the compatibility with multiplication that will be needed below; see also (Linckelmann 2013, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[Linckelmann2013](#ref-Linckelmann2013)]. For finite modules $M,N$ over a symmetric algebra $G$, it says that $$D\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{G}(M,N)\simeq\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{G}(N,M[-1]),
 \qquad D=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{k}(-,k),
 \label{cone:duality}$$ naturally in $N$. Here is a direct proof with our indexing conventions. Choose a complete resolution $Q$ of $M$, so that $M=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(Q_1\to Q_0)$. For a finite projective $Q_i$, symmetry gives a natural isomorphism $$D\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(Q_i,N)\simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,Q_i).$$ It suffices to check this for $Q_i=G$, where it is the isomorphism $DG\simeq G$, and then to pass to finite sums and summands. Dualizing the cochain complex $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(Q,N)$ therefore gives the chain complex $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_G(N,Q)$. Its homology at zero consists of maps into $\ker(Q_0\to Q_{-1})=\Omega M$, modulo maps through $Q_1$. These are exactly the projective factorizations, since maps from a projective to $\Omega M$ lift through $Q_1\twoheadrightarrow\Omega M$. Vector-space duality is exact, proving <a href="#cone:duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:duality">[cone:duality]</a> and its naturality.

In particular, <a id="cone:negative-duality"></a>$$DH^a\simeq H^{-a-1}.
\label{cone:negative-duality}$$ For $m\geq0$, let $\mathcal P_m$ denote the degree-$m$ part of the polynomial ring $k[\tau_1,\tau_2]$ when each variable is assigned degree one. Thus <a id="cone:support"></a>$$H^{3m}=\mathcal P_m,\qquad H^{-3m-1}=D\mathcal P_m\quad(m\geq0),
 \label{cone:support}$$ and all other groups vanish.

These identifications also determine the action of the two classes in negative degrees. Postcomposition by $\tau_i[a]$ gives $H^a\to H^{a+3}$. By naturality of <a href="#cone:duality" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:duality">[cone:duality]</a>, its transpose, after shifting the source back to $X$, is $$H^{-a-4}\longrightarrow H^{-a-1},\qquad
 g\longmapsto g[3]\circ\tau_i.$$ When $a\leq-4$, the last map is multiplication by $\tau_i$ in the polynomial ring <a href="#cone:positive-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:positive-ring">[cone:positive-ring]</a>. The action within negative degrees is therefore the dual of polynomial multiplication. There is no nonzero product crossing from negative to nonnegative degree: the only nonzero possible source is $H^{-1}$, and its target is $H^2=0$.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### Finite bimodules from projective tails

We will form the two cones as ordinary complexes before passing to stable bimodules. We give the passage explicitly to retain finite representatives and exact tensor evaluation.

Bimodules attached to Hochschild classes and their iterated tensor products have a precursor in the support-variety constructions of Erdmann, Holloway, Snashall, Solberg and Taillefer (Erdmann et al. 2004, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[ErdmannEtAl2003](#ref-ErdmannEtAl2003)]. Here we compute the full stable Hom profile directly, including negative degrees and the top attaching map.

If $V$ is a bounded below complex of finite projective $R$-modules that is exact in sufficiently high degrees, put <a id="cone:tail-definition"></a>$$\langle V\rangle=
 \bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(V_{N+1}\longrightarrow V_N)\bigr)[N]
 \label{cone:tail-definition}$$ in the stable category, with $N$ sufficiently large. This does not depend on $N$: writing $V_N^{\mathrm c}$ for this cokernel, exactness gives $$0\longrightarrow V_{N+1}^{\mathrm c}
 \longrightarrow V_N\longrightarrow V_N^{\mathrm c}
 \longrightarrow0.$$ Thus $V_{N+1}^{\mathrm c}\simeq V_N^{\mathrm c}[-1]$ stably. Chain maps defined in sufficiently high degrees induce maps on these cokernels, compatibly with increasing $N$ and with shifts. A chain homotopy changes the bottom induced map by a factorization through a projective, so homotopic tail maps induce the same stable map.

For a degreewise split short exact sequence of such complexes $0\to B\to V\to D\to0$, their cokernels at $N$ form a short exact sequence once $H_{N+1}(D)=0$. Indeed, a possible extra relation in the $B$-cokernel is obtained by lifting a cycle in $D_{N+1}$. That cycle is a boundary; subtracting a lifted boundary leaves a relation already in $B$. The resulting stable sequence has the usual long exact stable Hom sequence: apply $\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}$ from a complete projective resolution to the short exact sequence and use its cohomology description of stable Hom. If the cross term of the differential is $r:D_j\to B_{j-1}$, the connecting stable map $\langle D\rangle\to\langle B\rangle[1]$ is induced by $r$. This follows by lifting along the degreewise splitting and taking the $B$-component of the differential, which is precisely $r$.

Call a bimodule *side-projective* if it is projective separately as a left and as a right $A$-module. Finite stable shifts of a finite side-projective bimodule have finite side-projective representatives. For a syzygy, take a finite $R$-projective surjection; its kernel sequence splits on each side because its quotient is side-projective. For a cosyzygy, take a finite $R$-projective-injective embedding; its cokernel sequence splits on each side because its submodule is side-injective, by symmetry of $A$. Likewise a stable map $M\to N$ between such bimodules has a finite side-projective cone: represent the map by $f$, embed $M$ into a finite $R$-projective $I$, and take the cokernel of $(f,i):M\to N\oplus I$. Again the sequence splits on each side.

<a id="stmt-cone-finite"></a>
<div id="cone:finite" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11**. *Let $P$ be the bimodule bar resolution of $T$ and let $p:P\to P[3]$ be the selected comparison map. Form the ordinary complex $$K=\operatorname{Cone}(p:P[-3]\to P)
       \otimes_{k}\operatorname{Cone}(p:P[-3]\to P).$$ Then $K$ has finite projective $R$-terms and is exact in degrees greater than zero. For every $N\geq1$, its cokernel $C_N=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(K_{N+1}\to K_N)$ is finite and side-projective. Consequently $\mathcal C=\langle K\rangle$ has a finite side-projective module representative. The same conclusions hold for the first two filtration levels of $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cone-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-cone-finite</summary>

<a id="cone:cells"></a>
*Proof.* Write $P^{\mathrm{tot}}=P\otimes_{k}P$. In each total degree there are only finitely many summands, each an external tensor of projective bimodules; these are projective over $R$. The complex $P^{\mathrm{tot}}$ resolves $A$. Label the four cone cells by subsets $S\subseteq\{1,2\}$, where $i\in S$ means that the shifted cell is selected in cone factor $i$. The $S$-cell is $P^{\mathrm{tot}}[-2|S|]$; its differential to the cell $S\setminus\{i\}$ is the shifted comparison map $p_i$. Filtering by $|S|$ gives the three associated levels $$P^{\mathrm{tot}},\qquad
 (P^{\mathrm{tot}}[-2])^{\oplus2},\qquad
 P^{\mathrm{tot}}[-4].
 \label{cone:cells}$$ The cell homology is supported at $0,-2,-4$, respectively. The filtration long exact sequences therefore show that $K$ is exact in positive degrees.

For $N\geq1$, the cokernel filtration is exact by the preceding observation. Its factors, with multiplicities $1,2,1$, are $$\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}\bigl(P^{\mathrm{tot}}_{N+2r+1}
       \to P^{\mathrm{tot}}_{N+2r}\bigr),\qquad r=0,1,2.$$ The augmented resolution of $A$ splits on either $A$-side, since the regular module is projective; inductively all its syzygies are projective on each side. Thus these filtration factors are side-projective. Their extensions split on each side, proving the same property for $C_N$. Finally $C_N[N]$ has a finite side-projective representative by the shift construction above. In particular one may take $N=1$ and one finite cosyzygy of $C_1$. ◻

</details>

Forming the external tensor of ordinary complexes gives all four cells of <a href="#cone:cells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:cells">[cone:cells]</a> as projective resolutions of shifts of the regular $A$-bimodule. We pass to their stable representatives only after this construction.

Evaluation on a finite left module means tensoring over $A$ on the right. Tensoring with a side-projective bimodule is an exact functor of left $A$-modules and preserves projectives. Every $R$-projective evaluates to an $A$-projective, since a free bimodule evaluates to a module of the form $A\otimes_{k}V$. For evaluation at a fixed module, the right-split sequences used above remain exact. Their evaluations therefore preserve the stable shifts and long exact Hom sequences in the construction. Accordingly the filtration <a href="#cone:cells" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:cells">[cone:cells]</a>, after evaluation on $X$, has stable cells $$X,\qquad X[-2]^{\oplus2},\qquad X[-4].$$ Its adjacent connecting maps are the shifted actions of $\tau_1,\tau_2$: they are induced by the original bimodule comparison maps tensored with $X$. This specifies the attaching maps whose Hom groups we now compute.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### The Hom profile and its top projection

<a id="stmt-cone-profile"></a>
<div id="cone:profile" class="proposition">

<a id="cone:top-projection"></a>
**Proposition 12**. *For the finite side-projective bimodule $\mathcal C$ of Lemma <a href="#cone:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:finite">11</a>, put $$W^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,(\mathcal C\otimes_A X)[a]).$$ For every integer $a$, $$W^a=\begin{cases}k,&a=0\text{ or }3,\\0,&a\notin\{0,3\}.
 \end{cases}$$ Moreover the projection to the top cell induces an isomorphism $$W^3\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H^{-1}.
 \label{cone:top-projection}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cone-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-cone-profile</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\mathcal C_{\leq1}$ denote the first two filtration levels, represented by their projective tails as above. In total cohomological degree $a$, the Hom groups of the three associated levels are $$B^a=H^a,\qquad D^a=(H^{a-2})^2,\qquad E^a=H^{a-4}.$$ The adjacent connecting maps are $$d_a:D^a\longrightarrow B^{a+1},\qquad
 d_a(u,v)=\tau_1u+\tau_2v,$$ $$d'_a:E^a\longrightarrow D^{a+1},\qquad
 d'_a(z)=(\tau_2z,\tau_1z).$$ The order in the column records that deleting the first slot from the full cell leaves the second singleton, and conversely.

<a id="cone:koszul"></a>
<a id="cone:koszul-consequences"></a>
We need the degreewise exact Koszul sequence $$0\longrightarrow \mathcal P_{m-1}
 \xrightarrow{(\tau_2,\tau_1)^t}\mathcal P_m^{\oplus2}
 \xrightarrow{(\tau_1,\tau_2)}\mathcal P_{m+1}
 \longrightarrow0\qquad(m\geq0),
 \label{cone:koszul}$$ where $\mathcal P_j=0$ for $j<0$. The row is surjective because every positive-degree monomial is divisible by one of the variables. The column is injective, and coprimality of the variables shows that every relation $\tau_1u+\tau_2v=0$ is of the form $(u,v)=(\tau_2w,\tau_1w)$. On negative groups, dualize <a href="#cone:koszul" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:koszul">[cone:koszul]</a> and interchange its two middle components. By the multiplication calculation above this gives exactly the sequence through $D^a$ there. At the seam its terminal zero space is the actual group $H^2=0$. We obtain, in all degrees, $$\begin{aligned}
 \ker d_a&=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}d'_{a-1},\nonumber\\
 \mathop{\mathrm{coker}}d_{a-1}&=
 \begin{cases}k,&a=0,\\0,&a\ne0,\end{cases}
 &\ker d'_a&=
 \begin{cases}k=H^{-1},&a=3,\\0,&a\ne3.
 \end{cases}
 \label{cone:koszul-consequences}
\end{aligned}$$ For the column kernel, its maps on $D\mathcal P_m$ for $m\geq1$ are dual to the surjective positive row and hence injective. Its remaining source is $D\mathcal P_0=H^{-1}$, whose two products vanish in $H^2$.

<a id="cone:first-les"></a>
It remains to check that the actual filtration has no further effect on these scalar classes. Put $$K_1^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,(\mathcal C_{\leq 1}\otimes_A X)[a]).$$ The first filtration long exact sequence gives $$0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}d_{a-1}\longrightarrow K_1^a
 \xrightarrow{q_a}\ker d_a\longrightarrow0.
 \label{cone:first-les}$$ The top-cell connecting map is $r_a:E^a\to K_1^{a+1}$, and its projection satisfies $q_{a+1}r_a=d'_a$. Thus $\ker r_a\subseteq\ker d'_a$, so it can be nonzero only when $a=3$. At that index $K_1^4=0$ by <a href="#cone:first-les" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:first-les">[cone:first-les]</a>, since $D^4=(H^2)^2=0$. Hence $\ker r_3=E^3=H^{-1}$.

The image of $r_{a-1}$ surjects onto $\ker d_a$ by <a href="#cone:koszul-consequences" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:koszul-consequences">[cone:koszul-consequences]</a>. When $a\ne0$, the left term of <a href="#cone:first-les" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:first-les">[cone:first-les]</a> vanishes, so $r_{a-1}$ is surjective. When $a=0$, that sequence gives $K_1^0=k$, whereas the only incoming source is $E^{-1}=H^{-5}=0$. Therefore $\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}r_{a-1}$ is $k$ exactly at $a=0$ and zero elsewhere.

Finally the second filtration long exact sequence gives $$0\longrightarrow\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}r_{a-1}\longrightarrow W^a
 \longrightarrow\ker r_a\longrightarrow0.$$ This proves the asserted groups. At $a=3$ its left term vanishes and its right term is all of $E^3=H^{-1}$, so the actual top-cell projection is an isomorphism, proving <a href="#cone:top-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="cone:top-projection">[cone:top-projection]</a>. ◻

</details>

The two exact sequences in the proof account for the complete filtration, including any attaching map not determined by its adjacent component. We have therefore obtained the required two-scalar profile together with a concrete way to detect its degree-three class: after shifting the cone by three, it suffices to project to $X[-1]$. The next construction supplies maps with a nonzero projection there.

<a id="sec:lift"></a>
### Twisted bimodules and a lift to the two cones

We now construct two stable bimodule maps whose evaluations generate the one-dimensional degree-three group of the cone calculation. Their sources will have distinct twisting actions. The essential point is a chain-level lift: we give the homotopies in every degree and identify its projection to the top cell.

<a id="lift:extension"></a>
For a nonzero scalar $H\in k$, let $h=h_H$ be the automorphism of $T$ that fixes $C$ and multiplies $DC$ by $H$. Write $T_h$ for the bimodule with its usual left action and right action $x\cdot a=xh(a)$. Put $$U=T_h,\qquad Q=P_h,\qquad
 U_H=A_{h\otimes h},\qquad Q^{\mathrm{tot}}=Q\otimes_kQ.$$ Thus $Q$ is the right-twisted bar resolution of $U$, with augmentation $\epsilon:Q_0\to U$. A formula on its bar generators extends by $$\psi\bigl(a_0[a_1|\cdots|a_i]a_{i+1}\bigr)
 =a_0\psi([a_1|\cdots|a_i])h^{-1}(a_{i+1}).
 \label{lift:extension}$$ Here the displayed terminal coefficient is its underlying element of $T$. This convention makes $\psi$ right linear from the twisted source to an untwisted target.

<a id="stmt-lift-main"></a>
<div id="lift:main" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13**. *For each $H\in k^\times$, there is a stable bimodule map $$f_H:U_H\longrightarrow\mathcal C[3]$$ whose composite with the top-cell projection $\mathcal C[3]\to A[-1]$ is represented by the bimodule map $\beta_H:U_H\to\Omega A\subset P_0\otimes P_0$ determined by $$\beta_H(1)=\left(\sum_w h(w)\otimes_I w^*\right)
 \otimes\left(\sum_w h(w)\otimes_I w^*\right),$$ where each sum is over the letter basis of $T$. Under the canonical identification $U_H\otimes_A X\simeq X$, its projection after evaluation sends $$1\longmapsto H^2(f^*\otimes f^*)
 \quad\text{in}\quad
 \Omega X=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)(f\otimes f)\subset A(f\otimes f).$$ This evaluated map is nonzero modulo maps factoring through projectives. In particular, $f_HX$ is nonzero in $\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,\mathcal C X[3])$.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### A twisted trace-dual tensor

<a id="lift:casimir"></a>
For a basis letter $w$ of $T$, write $w^*$ for its trace-dual letter. In $P_0=T\otimes_I T$, set $$\xi_H=\sum_{w\text{ in the basis of }T}h(w)\otimes_I w^*.
 \label{lift:casimir}$$ The trace-dual coevaluation identity is standard; see (Broué 2009, Proposition 3.3) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Broue2009](#ref-Broue2009)]. The associative symmetric trace gives the ordinary coevaluation identity $a\sum_w w\otimes w^*=\sum_w w\otimes w^*a$. Applying $h$ to the first factor and projecting to $\otimes_I$ gives $$h(a)\xi_H=\xi_Ha.$$ Consequently $b:U\to P_0$, defined by $b(x)=x\xi_H$, is a bimodule map.

<a id="lift:endpoint"></a>
We need the stronger fact that $b$ takes values in $\ker\pi$, where $\pi:P_0\to T$ is multiplication. For a lower-case basis letter $w$ of $C$, the product $ww^*$ is the dual of its left vertex idempotent, and $w^*w$ is the dual of its right vertex idempotent. Indeed $(ww^*)(c)=w^*(cw)$; the positive grading makes this zero for $c\in\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C$, and the idempotent values determine the assertion. The row dimensions of $C$ are $6,4$, and its column dimensions are also $6,4$. Separating the lower-case and upper-case letters therefore gives $$\pi\xi_H=\sum_{w\text{ lower-case}}ww^*
       +H\sum_{w\text{ lower-case}}w^*w=0$$ in characteristic two. The same calculation on each left vertex $r\in\{e,f\}$ gives $$\sum_{\substack{w\in\mathfrak r\text{ a basis letter}\\
                         \mathrm{left}(w)=r}}h(w)w^*=r^*.
 \label{lift:endpoint}$$ Here the full sum is zero and the omitted idempotent term is $rr^*=r^*$.

Taking the external square, define <a id="lift:beta"></a>$$\beta_H=b\otimes_kb:
 U_H\longrightarrow\ker(P^{\mathrm{tot}}_0\to A)=\Omega A,
 \qquad P^{\mathrm{tot}}=P\otimes_kP.
 \label{lift:beta}$$ Its image lies in the indicated kernel because $\pi b=0$. The remaining task is to lift this map through the top-cell projection.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Two homotopies on the bar resolution

Let $D_b:Q\to P$ be $b\epsilon$ in degree zero and zero in every positive degree. It is a chain map. A homotopy for $D_b$ will supply a comparison representing $\beta_H$ in the top cell. Define maps $B_i:Q_i\to P_{i+1}$ for every $i\geq0$ by <a id="lift:Bformula"></a>$$B_i([a_1|\cdots|a_i])
 =\sum_{w\in\mathfrak r\text{ a basis letter}}
     [a_1|\cdots|a_i|h(w)]w^*,
 \label{lift:Bformula}$$ retaining only composable terms and extending by <a href="#lift:extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:extension">[lift:extension]</a>.

<a id="stmt-lift-b"></a>
<div id="lift:B" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14**. *On the nonnegative resolutions, with $B_{-1}=0$, one has $$dB+Bd=D_b.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-b"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-b</summary>

<a id="lift:projected-casimir"></a>
*Proof.* For $a\in\mathfrak r$ with right vertex $r$, project the first factor of $a\xi_H=\xi_Hh^{-1}(a)$ to $\mathfrak r$ along $I$. Removing the idempotent terms leaves $$\sum_{w\in\mathfrak r}ah(w)\otimes w^*
 +\sum_{w\in\mathfrak r}h(w)\otimes w^*h^{-1}(a)
 =a\otimes r^*,
 \label{lift:projected-casimir}$$ with the appropriate vertex restrictions in each sum. On the left side of the original identity, the omitted term is $a\otimes r^*$; on its right side the omitted first factors lie in $I$ and disappear under the projection.

For $i>0$, expand $dB_i+B_{i-1}d$ on a bar generator. All multiplications strictly before the old final bar cancel in pairs. The two terms adjacent to the old final letter have sum $[a_1|\cdots|a_i]r^*$ by <a href="#lift:projected-casimir" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:projected-casimir">[lift:projected-casimir]</a>. The last multiplication in $dB_i$ is exactly the same expression by <a href="#lift:endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:endpoint">[lift:endpoint]</a>, so these terms also cancel.

At degree zero, on the generator $r[]r$, the differential gives $$dB_0(r[]r)=
 \sum_{\substack{w\in\mathfrak r\\\mathrm{left}(w)=r}}h(w)[]w^*
 +r[]\sum_{\substack{w\in\mathfrak r\\\mathrm{left}(w)=r}}h(w)w^*.$$ The second term is $r[]r^*$ by <a href="#lift:endpoint" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:endpoint">[lift:endpoint]</a>. It restores the omitted idempotent term of the full tensor <a href="#lift:casimir" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:casimir">[lift:casimir]</a>; the result is $r\xi_H=b\epsilon(r[]r)$. Bimodule linearity proves the identity on every degree-zero element. ◻

</details>

We now use $B$ to represent the prescribed top projection. In $K[3]$, the cell indexed by $\{1,2\}$ is $P^{\mathrm{tot}}[-1]$. Put $V=B\otimes D_b$, which raises the underlying total degree by one, as this shift requires. Lemma <a href="#lift:B" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:B">14</a> gives $$dV+Vd=(b\otimes b)\epsilon^{\mathrm{tot}},$$ where the right side is supported in degree zero. Thus $dV_0=\beta_H\epsilon^{\mathrm{tot}}_0$, and at positive indices $V$ satisfies the comparison equation. The complex with terms $P^{\mathrm{tot}}_{i+1}$ and augmentation to $\Omega A$ resolves $\Omega A$. Consequently $V$ is a comparison lifting $\beta_H$.

To extend $V$ from the top cell to $K[3]$, we must account for the two attaching maps. Deleting slot 1 contributes $pB\otimes D_b$ to the cell indexed by $\{2\}$. The suffix comparison $p:P\to P[3]$ from the finite cochain data lowers the underlying bar degree by three, so $pD_b=0$. The contribution from deleting slot 2 is therefore zero. It remains to cancel $pB\otimes D_b$ by a component in cell $\{2\}$. Lemma <a href="#lift:B" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:B">14</a> shows that $pB:Q\to P[2]$ is a chain map. The additional boundary identity <a href="#coc:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="coc:boundary">[coc:boundary]</a> supplies its nullhomotopy; cocycle closure by itself would not suffice.

<a id="stmt-lift-g"></a>
<div id="lift:G" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15**. *There are bimodule maps $G_i:Q_i\to P_{i-1}$ for all $i\geq0$ such that $$pB=dG+Gd.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-g"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-g</summary>

<a id="lift:boundary"></a>
*Proof.* Let $z':Q_1\to T$ be the cochain given by $z'([a])=z_H(a)$ from Lemma <a href="#coc:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="coc:data">7</a>, with extension <a href="#lift:extension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:extension">[lift:extension]</a>. Its boundary on $[a|b]$ is $$az'(b)+z'(ab)+z'(a)h^{-1}(b).$$ By identity <a href="#coc:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="coc:boundary">[coc:boundary]</a>, this equals $\sum_{w\in\mathfrak r}p(a,b,h(w))w^*$, which is the augmentation of $(pB)_2$. Hence $$\pi(pB)_2=z'd:Q_2\longrightarrow T.
 \label{lift:boundary}$$

Set $G_0=0$ and choose $G_1:Q_1\to P_0$ with $\pi G_1=z'$. The choice is possible because $Q_1$ is projective and $\pi$ is surjective. By <a href="#lift:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:boundary">[lift:boundary]</a>, the map $(pB)_2+G_1d$ has image in $\ker\pi=\mathop{\mathrm{im}}(d:P_1\to P_0)$. Projectivity of $Q_2$ therefore supplies $G_2$ with $dG_2=(pB)_2+G_1d$.

Suppose $i\geq3$ and the homotopy equation has been established below $i$. Since $pB$ is a chain map, $$d\bigl((pB)_i+G_{i-1}d\bigr)
 =(pB)_{i-1}d+
       \bigl((pB)_{i-1}+G_{i-2}d\bigr)d=0.$$ Exactness of $P$ in positive degree and projectivity of $Q_i$ allow this map to be lifted through $d:P_{i-1}\to P_{i-2}$, giving $G_i$. This proves the equation for every $i\geq2$. At $i=0,1$ both sides are zero, since $pB$ has negative target index and $G_0=0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### The tail map and its evaluation

The homotopy $G$ supplies the required correction in the singleton cell $\{2\}$, whose shift in $K[3]$ is $1$. Together with the top component $V$, it defines a graded map $\Phi:Q^{\mathrm{tot}}\to K[3]$: <a id="lift:tail"></a>$$\Phi_{\{1,2\}}=B\otimes D_b,\qquad
 \Phi_{\{2\}}=G\otimes D_b,\qquad
 \Phi_{\{1\}}=\Phi_{\varnothing}=0.
 \label{lift:tail}$$ The second component lowers underlying degree by one, as the singleton shift requires; the first is the comparison $V$ constructed above.

In the top cell, the chain defect $d\Phi+\Phi d$ is $D_b\otimes D_b$. In cell $\{2\}$, deletion of slot 1 gives $pB\otimes D_b$, canceled by $(dG+Gd)\otimes D_b$. Deleting slot 2 from either nonzero component gives zero because $pD_b=0$. Thus the only defect is $D_b\otimes D_b$, supported in total source degree zero. The map therefore commutes with the differential in every positive total degree. Passing to high cokernels, at a common index at least $4$ as in Lemma <a href="#cone:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:finite">11</a>, gives $f_H:U_H\to\mathcal C[3]$.

The top component is still $V$, so the resulting stable projection is $\beta_H$, as identified above.

<a id="stmt-lift-evaluation"></a>
<div id="lift:evaluation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16**. *Under $U_H\otimes_A X\simeq X$, the evaluated map $\beta_H X$ sends $1$ to $H^2(f^*\otimes f^*)$ in $\Omega X\subset A(f\otimes f)$. It does not factor through a projective $A$-module.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lift-evaluation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lift-evaluation</summary>

*Proof.* In one slot, tensoring <a href="#lift:casimir" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="lift:casimir">[lift:casimir]</a> with the simple leaves only the term whose right coefficient is $f$. It is the term $w=f^*$, so its value is $Hf^*$. Taking the external square gives the stated value. The cover sequence remains exact on evaluation because it splits on the right. Its evaluated degree-zero term is $A(f\otimes f)$, the projective cover of $X$, with kernel $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)(f\otimes f)$.

Suppose a map $X\to\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)(f\otimes f)$ factors through a projective. It then factors through a finite free module. Every component of a map $X\to A$ has image in the left socle, since $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)X=0$. In a symmetric algebra the left socle also annihilates the radical on the right: if $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)u=0$, then, for $j\in\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)$ and $a\in A$, $$\operatorname{tr}(auj)=\operatorname{tr}(jau)=0,$$ so nondegeneracy of the trace gives $uj=0$. A map $A\to\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)(f\otimes f)$ is right multiplication by its value at $1$, which lies in $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(A)$, and hence kills the left socle. Every such factorization is therefore zero. Our displayed map has the nonzero value $H^2(f^*\otimes f^*)$, proving the claim. ◻

</details>

By Proposition <a href="#cone:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:profile">12</a>, the top-cell projection is an isomorphism on the degree-three group of the cone calculation. Lemma <a href="#lift:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:evaluation">16</a> therefore proves that $f_HX$ is nonzero, completing Proposition <a href="#lift:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:main">13</a>. We have obtained the two required maps by taking $H=H_1,H_2$; the next section combines them into a finite bimodule.

<a id="branch:section"></a>
### Two twists and a finite bimodule fiber

We now construct the finite bimodule and the stable map required by Proposition <a href="#conv:proposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conv:proposition">2</a>. The two twists will act differently on every positive self-extension of $X$, while agreeing on its identity. The fiber of their maps to $\mathcal C[3]$ turns this distinction into the comparison isomorphisms used in that proposition.

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### A finite representative and an exact sequence

Write $R=A^e$. Lemma <a href="#cone:finite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:finite">11</a> provides the finite bimodule $$C_1=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(K_2\longrightarrow K_1),
 \qquad \mathcal C=C_1[1]$$ in the stable category of $R$. It is projective on both $A$-sides. To fix actual finite representatives for its shifts, for a finite left $R$-module $M$ use the injection <a id="branch:finite-embedding"></a>$$\label{branch:finite-embedding}
 j_M:M\longrightarrow I_R(M):=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{k}(R,M),
 \qquad j_M(m)(t)=tm,
 \qquad (r\varphi)(t)=\varphi(tr).$$ Evaluation at $1$ proves injectivity. Since $R$ is symmetric, $I_R(M)\cong DR\otimes_{k}M\cong R\otimes_{k}M$ as left $R$-modules, so this is a finite projective module. Put $\Sigma_RM=\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}j_M$. When $M$ is projective on each $A$-side, the sequence defining $\Sigma_RM$ splits on each side: $A$ is self-injective, so $M$ is injective on either side. Thus $\Sigma_RM$ remains projective on both sides and represents $M[1]$.

In particular, fix the actual finite bimodule <a id="branch:Y"></a>$$\label{branch:Y}
 \mathcal Y=\Sigma_R^4C_1.$$ It is projective on both sides and represents $\mathcal C[3]$. This fixes one target for both lifts of Proposition <a href="#lift:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:main">13</a>. Concretely, the tail map there at index $4$ induces $\Omega_R^4U_H\to C_1$, because $$\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}\bigl(K[3]_5\longrightarrow K[3]_4\bigr)
 =\mathop{\mathrm{coker}}(K_2\longrightarrow K_1).$$ Taking four cosyzygies gives the stable map $U_H\to \mathcal Y$, using $\Sigma_R^4\Omega_R^4U_H\cong U_H$ stably. It therefore has an actual bimodule-map representative between these finite modules.

For $i=1,2$, put $$\sigma_i=h_{H_i}\otimes h_{H_i},\qquad U_i=A_{\sigma_i}.$$ Thus $U_i$ has its usual left action and right action $u\cdot a=u\sigma_i(a)$. Each $U_i$ is free of rank one on both $A$-sides. The canonical identification $U_i\otimes_A X=X$ uses the fact that $\sigma_i$ fixes the simple character of $X$.

By Lemma <a href="#lift:evaluation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lift:evaluation">16</a>, the top projections of the evaluated lifts are $H_i^2\beta_0$ for one fixed nonzero class $\beta_0\in\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,X[-1])$. By Proposition <a href="#cone:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:profile">12</a>, the top projection $$W^3=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,\mathcal C X[3])
 \longrightarrow \underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,X[-1])$$ is an isomorphism. Rescale the two lifts by $H_i^{-2}$, and choose actual representatives $g_i:U_i\to \mathcal Y$ of these rescaled maps. Their evaluations are the same nonzero element $w\in W^3$.

<a id="branch:finite-fiber"></a>
Take the finite free $R$-module $Q=R\otimes_{k}\mathcal Y$ and its surjection $\pi:Q\to \mathcal Y$, $\pi(r\otimes y)=ry$. Define $F$ by the exact sequence $$\label{branch:finite-fiber}
 0\longrightarrow F\longrightarrow U_1\oplus U_2\oplus Q
 \xrightarrow{\ (g_1,g_2,\pi)\ }\mathcal Y\longrightarrow0,$$ and let $\rho_i:F\to U_i$ be the two projections. This is a construction of finite modules: $C_1$ is a cokernel of two finite terms, four finite cokernels give $\mathcal Y$, and one finite kernel gives $F$. Since $\mathcal Y$ is projective on each side, <a href="#branch:finite-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:finite-fiber">[branch:finite-fiber]</a> splits on each side. Consequently $F$ is projective as both a left and a right $A$-module.

<a id="branch:evaluated-triangle"></a>
Tensoring <a href="#branch:finite-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:finite-fiber">[branch:finite-fiber]</a> with $X$ preserves exactness. Furthermore, $Q\otimes_AX$ is projective over $A$: evaluation of a free $A^e$-module is a free $A$-module, and evaluation preserves direct summands. Thus the evaluated stable triangle is $$\label{branch:evaluated-triangle}
 FX\longrightarrow X\oplus X\xrightarrow{\ (w,w)\ }
 \mathcal C X[3]\longrightarrow FX[1].$$ Here and below $FM$ means $F\otimes_AM$. Tensoring with $F$ is exact because $F$ is right projective; it preserves projectives because $F$ is left projective. It therefore acts on stable maps compatibly with shifts. The actual maps $\rho_i$ give natural transformations from this functor to tensoring with $U_i$.

<a id="stmt-branch-profile"></a>
<div id="branch:profile" class="lemma">

<a id="branch:positive-projection"></a>
**Lemma 17**. *Put $V^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,FX[a])$ for each integer $a$. There is a unique $v\in V^0$ whose two projections are $\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_X$. It spans $V^0$. For every integer $a>0$, projection induces an isomorphism $$\label{branch:positive-projection}
  (\rho_1[a],\rho_2[a]):V^a\xrightarrow{\sim}
  (H^a)^2,
  \qquad H^a=\underline{\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}}_{A}(X,X[a]).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-branch-profile"><summary>Proof of stmt-branch-profile</summary>

*Proof.* Applying stable Hom from $X$ to <a href="#branch:evaluated-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:evaluated-triangle">[branch:evaluated-triangle]</a> gives $$(H^{a-1})^2\longrightarrow W^{a+2}
 \longrightarrow V^a\longrightarrow(H^a)^2
 \longrightarrow W^{a+3}.$$ At $a=0$, the first relevant group is $W^2=0$, and the last map is $k^2\to W^3$, $(c,d)\mapsto(c+d)w$. It is surjective, so $V^0$ identifies with the diagonal line in $k^2$. This proves the assertion about $v$.

At $a=1$, the preceding map $(H^0)^2\to W^3$ is precisely this surjection. It kills the potential connecting term from $W^3$ to $V^1$, and $W^4=0$. Therefore <a href="#branch:positive-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:positive-projection">[branch:positive-projection]</a> is an isomorphism in degree $1$ as well. For $a\ge2$, both $W^{a+2}$ and $W^{a+3}$ vanish by Proposition <a href="#cone:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cone:profile">12</a>, giving the isomorphism directly. ◻

</details>

We have obtained an actual finite bimodule $F$ and a nonzero stable map $v:X\to FX$. It remains to compare tensoring an extension with $F$ and composing that extension with $v$.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### The twist eigenvalues and the comparison map

<a id="stmt-branch-twist"></a>
<div id="branch:twist" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18**. *For $H\in\{H_1,H_2\}$, tensoring with $U_H=A_{h_H\otimes h_H}$, under its canonical identification on $X$, acts on the whole space $H^{3m}$ as multiplication by $H^{-m}$ for every integer $m\ge0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-branch-twist"><summary>Proof of stmt-branch-twist</summary>

*Proof.* The inverse automorphism in this formula follows from the right-twist convention. For an automorphism $h$ of $T$ and a left $T$-module $M$, the balanced map $$T_h\otimes_TM\longrightarrow {}_{h^{-1}}M,
 \qquad a\otimes m\longmapsto h^{-1}(a)m$$ is an isomorphism; the target action is $b\cdot m=h^{-1}(b)m$. For $M=s$ it is the canonical simple identification.

In the normalized bar resolution of $s$, a comparison into its $T_h$-tensor image applies $h^{-1}$ to every bar letter. It commutes with the differential because $h^{-1}$ preserves products; the first multiplication agrees by the balancing relation for the right-twisted factor, and the last action agrees because $h$ fixes the character of $s$. The simple-valued degree-three cocycle obtained from $p$ has exactly one dual-ideal input in each nonzero component. Indeed $p$ lowers the dual-letter weight by one, and only weight-zero outputs can act nontrivially on $s$. The bar comparison therefore multiplies this cochain, and hence its class $\tau$, by $H^{-1}$.

The tensor functor preserves Yoneda products. In the two-factor algebra $A$, both factors of one branch use the same $H$, so both $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ acquire the factor $H^{-1}$. Every basis monomial $\tau_1^r\tau_2^{m-r}$ of $H^{3m}$ acquires $H^{-m}$. Linearity gives the assertion on the entire homogeneous piece. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-branch-comparison"></a>
<div id="branch:comparison" class="proposition">

<a id="branch:delta"></a>
**Proposition 19**. *The finite bimodule $F$ in <a href="#branch:finite-fiber" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:finite-fiber">[branch:finite-fiber]</a> is projective on both $A$-sides, and the map $v$ of Lemma <a href="#branch:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="branch:profile">17</a> satisfies the following conditions. For every integer $a\ge0$, define $$\label{branch:delta}
  \delta^a:H^a\oplus H^a\longrightarrow V^a,
  \qquad (g,j)\longmapsto F(g)v+v[a]j.$$ Then $\delta^0$ is surjective with a one-dimensional kernel, and $\delta^a$ is an isomorphism for every integer $a>0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-branch-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-branch-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Finiteness and both projectivity assertions were proved in the construction. The actual bimodule projections $\rho_i$ are natural with respect to tensoring module maps and their stable shifts. Since $\rho_i v=\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_X$, they give $$\rho_i[a]F(g)v=U_i(g),\qquad
 \rho_i[a]v[a]j=j.$$ For $a=0$, $k$-linearity therefore gives $\delta^0(c\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_X,d\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_X)=(c+d)v$. This is onto and has the diagonal line as its kernel.

<a id="branch:matrix"></a>
Let $a=3m>0$. Under <a href="#branch:positive-projection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:positive-projection">[branch:positive-projection]</a> and Lemma <a href="#branch:twist" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="branch:twist">18</a>, the map <a href="#branch:delta" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:delta">[branch:delta]</a> is $$\label{branch:matrix}
 (g,j)\longmapsto
 \bigl(H_1^{-m}g+j,\ H_2^{-m}g+j\bigr).$$ It is a $2\times2$ scalar matrix tensored with the identity of $H^{3m}$; that space has dimension $m+1$. The scalar matrix has determinant $H_1^{-m}+H_2^{-m}\ne0$. Otherwise $H_1^m=H_2^m$, contrary to algebraic independence: the two distinct monomials remain distinct in the fraction field of $\mathbb F_2[q,H_1,H_2]$. This argument holds for every positive integer $m$, including those divisible by the characteristic. Hence <a href="#branch:matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="branch:matrix">[branch:matrix]</a> is invertible.

If $a>0$ is not divisible by $3$, then $H^a=0$, and Lemma <a href="#branch:profile" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="branch:profile">17</a> gives $V^a=0$. Thus $\delta^a$ is an isomorphism in these degrees too. ◻

</details>

The hypotheses of Proposition <a href="#conv:proposition" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="conv:proposition">2</a> are now verified for the finite symmetric algebra $A$, its finite module $X$, and the actual finite bimodule $F$ and stable map $v$. Applying that proposition to $$\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}A&0\\ F&A\end{pmatrix}$$ produces the required finite nonprojective Gorenstein-projective left module, with both positive Ext vanishings in every degree. In particular, the degree-one assertion uses the surjectivity at degree zero established in Proposition <a href="#branch:comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="branch:comparison">19</a>, in addition to the positive-degree invertibility.

<a id="sec:consequences"></a>
### The radical and extension of scalars

The construction has already supplied the two Ext vanishings and a finite nonprojective Gorenstein-projective module. We verify the remaining assertions of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-prop-radical"></a>
<div id="prop:radical" class="proposition">

**Proposition 20**. *The algebra $\Lambda$ is noncommutative, satisfies $\Lambda/\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}\Lambda\simeq k^8$, and has $\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}(\Lambda)^4\ne0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-radical"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-radical</summary>

*Proof.* Put $J_T=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}T$. Since $T=C\ltimes DC$ and $C/\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C\simeq k^2$, $$J_T=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C\oplus DC,\qquad T/J_T\simeq k^2.$$ The ideal $J_T\otimes T+T\otimes J_T$ in $A=T\otimes T$ is nilpotent: the two summand ideals commute, and each is nilpotent. Its quotient is $k^4$, so it equals $J_A=\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}A$. In the triangular algebra, the ideal $$J_\Lambda=\begin{pmatrix}J_A&0\\F&J_A\end{pmatrix}$$ is nilpotent as well. Indeed, a product has at most one off-diagonal factor, with powers of $J_A$ on its two sides; sufficiently long products vanish. Its quotient is $k^4\oplus k^4$. A nilpotent ideal with semisimple quotient is the radical, proving the split assertion and the count of simple modules.

Let $\chi:T\to k$ be the character of $s$. There are surjective algebra maps $$\Lambda\longrightarrow A
 \xrightarrow{\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_T\otimes\chi}T\longrightarrow C.$$ For a surjection of finite-dimensional algebras, the image of the radical is the radical of the quotient: the image is nilpotent, and the quotient by that image is a quotient of a semisimple algebra. In $C$, the multiplication table gives $$(ut)^2=(y+qx)^2=q(1+q)z\ne0.$$ Since $u,t\in\mathop{\mathrm{rad}}C$, the fourth radical power of $C$ is nonzero, and consequently so is that of $\Lambda$. Finally $eu=u$ and $ue=0$ in $C$, so $C$ and its covering algebra $\Lambda$ are noncommutative. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-basechange"></a>
<div id="prop:basechange" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21**. *For every field extension $K/k$, the algebra $\Lambda_K=K\otimes_k\Lambda$ and module $Z_K=K\otimes_kZ$ satisfy all conclusions of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> over $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-basechange"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-basechange</summary>

<a id="eq:basechange"></a>
*Proof.* For finite $\Lambda$-modules $M,N$, take a resolution of $M$ with finite projective terms. In each degree, finite projectivity gives $$K\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_\Lambda(P_i,N)
 \simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_{\Lambda_K}(K\otimes_kP_i,K\otimes_kN).$$ Flatness of $K$ over $k$ commutes with kernels and images, hence $$\label{eq:basechange}
 K\otimes_k\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Lambda^i(M,N)
 \simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_{\Lambda_K}^i(M_K,N_K)\qquad(i\geq0).$$ This proves both Ext vanishings. The totally acyclic complex defining $Z$ remains exact after tensoring with $K$; the same termwise Hom isomorphism, now with target $\Lambda$, shows that its dual complex over $\Lambda_K$ is exact. Thus $Z_K$ is Gorenstein-projective.

Choose a finite projective surjection $P\twoheadrightarrow Z$ with kernel $N$. It does not split, because $Z$ is nonprojective. Its class in $\mathop{\mathrm{Ext}}_\Lambda^1(Z,N)$ is nonzero and stays nonzero under <a href="#eq:basechange" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:basechange">[eq:basechange]</a>, since field extension is faithful. Thus the extended sequence does not split and $Z_K$ is nonprojective.

The radical ideal in Proposition <a href="#prop:radical" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:radical">20</a> stays nilpotent and has quotient $K^8$ after scalar extension, so it is the radical of $\Lambda_K$. Its nonzero fourth power stays nonzero. This also proves the split and radical assertions over $K$. ◻

</details>

<a id="app:cochain"></a>
### The complete degree-three cochain

This appendix specifies the finite cochain used in Lemma <a href="#coc:data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="coc:data">7</a>, including the coefficient identities that verify its properties. Lower-case letters are the basis of $C$ from <a href="#alg:C-corners" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:C-corners">[alg:C-corners]</a>; capital letters are the dual basis of $DC$. A four-letter entry $abcv$ in the table means that the indicated coefficient is the coefficient of $v$ in $p(a,b,c)$. All unlisted coefficients are zero. For example, the entry `tyJf` in the $q^3$ row means $p(t,y,J)=q^3f$. Each entry has composable radical inputs and an output with the same two endpoints.

<div class="center">

| coefficient | input and output letters                            |
|------------:|:----------------------------------------------------|
|       $q^2$ | `EuNT EyZX FNtU JtXZ NtEU NtXV XzJT ZxEY tEun tXuf` |
|       $q^2$ | `tXvn tXzj tuFn tuNf tvNn uFNT vNNT vtJu xEyz zJtx` |
|         $q$ | `EuUE EuVX EvVE ExXE EyYE EzZE FNnF FVvF FVxU FtTF` |
|         $q$ | `FtYU Ftun Ftxj JFtX JNtZ JUyX JVyZ JjEE JjTT JjXX` |
|         $q$ | `NFtU NNtV NUyU NVyV NjJN NjZV NnNN NnVV Ntuf Ntvn` |
|         $q$ | `Ntzj TtEE TtXX UuFF UuUU UvNF UvVU UxXU Uyun Uyxj` |
|         $q$ | `UzZU VvNN VvVV VxEU VxXV Vyuf Vyvn Vyzj VzZV XxEE` |
|         $q$ | `XxXX YuFT YuUY YvNT YvVY YxXY YyEE YyJJ YyTT YyXX` |
|         $q$ | `YyYY YyZZ YzZY ZxXZ ZzEE ZzJJ ZzTT ZzXX jJFF jJUU` |
|         $q$ | `jJjj jJnn jYyj jZvn jZzj nNFF nNUU ntTn tJjt tTNN` |
|         $q$ | `tTVV tTtt tYyt tZzt tzJn uFVX uFty uNFT uNUY uNte` |
|         $q$ | `uUyy uVye ujJu vNUE vNVX vNty vVyy vtTv xEuv xJFT` |
|         $q$ | `xJUY xJjx xJte xTtx xXuu xXvv xXyy xXzz xYyx xZye` |
|         $q$ | `xZzx xuFv xuNu xvNv yxJu yzJv zJNT zJVY zYyz zZuu` |
|         $q$ | `zZvv zZyy zZzz`                                    |
|       $q^3$ | `EyJT JtEY tEyj tyJf`                               |
|         $1$ | `JNjX JVzX NNjU NVzU NjYU Njun Njxj Ntxt TNtX TVyX` |
|         $1$ | `Vyxt VzYU Vzun Vzxj YuNJ YuVZ jYun juNn jxJn txTn` |
|         $1$ | `uNNJ uNVZ uNjy uVzy xJNJ xJVZ xTNT xTVY yxTv zYuv` |
|         $1$ | `zuNv zxJv`                                         |

</div>

There are 179 entries, with distinct three-letter inputs. In each entry the number of capital input letters is one more than the number of capital output letters. This proves the weight assertion.

<a id="coc:transpose-recurrence"></a>
Here are explicit finite recurrences for checking the remaining identities. They also specify how every zero entry participates in the calculation. Let $\mathcal B$ be the twenty-letter basis and $\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}=\mathcal B\setminus\{e,f\}$. Write $$ab=\sum_v\mu_{ab}^{v}v,
 \qquad p(a,b,c)=\sum_v p_{abc}^{v}v.$$ The lower-case coefficients $\mu$ are exactly <a href="#alg:C-products" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="alg:C-products">[alg:C-products]</a> and the idempotent actions. The remaining coefficients are determined, for lower-case $a,b,c$, by $$\mu_{a,b^*}^{c^*}=\mu_{ca}^{b},\qquad
 \mu_{b^*,a}^{c^*}=\mu_{ac}^{b},\qquad
 \mu_{a^*,b^*}^{v}=0.
\label{coc:transpose-recurrence}$$ Unspecified coefficients are zero. In particular every coefficient belongs to $\mathbb F_2[q]$.

For each $a,b,c,d\in\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}$ and $v\in\mathcal B$, substitution of the table and <a href="#coc:transpose-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="coc:transpose-recurrence">[coc:transpose-recurrence]</a> gives <a id="coc:closure-recurrence"></a>$$\begin{split}
0={}&\sum_{x\in\mathcal B}\bigl(
 \mu_{ax}^{v}p_{bcd}^{x}
 +\mu_{ab}^{x}p_{xcd}^{v}
 +\mu_{bc}^{x}p_{axd}^{v}\\
 &\hspace{35mm}
 +\mu_{cd}^{x}p_{abx}^{v}
 +p_{abc}^{x}\mu_{xd}^{v}\bigr).
\end{split}
\label{coc:closure-recurrence}$$ This is the coefficient of $v$ in the normalized Hochschild differential. One may check it on all $18^4=104\,976$ radical four-words; precisely $15\,250$ have composable endpoints, and the other equations vanish by the corners. Thus the finite calculation covers the whole relative bar term, not a selected collection of words.

For completeness, the boundary identity has an equally short coefficient check. Set $\epsilon_a=0$ on lower-case letters and $\epsilon_a=1$ on capital letters. For every basis letter put $$\gamma_a=
 \begin{cases}
 q,&a\in\{u,v,t,E,X,Y,Z,U,V\},\\
 0,&\text{otherwise}.
 \end{cases}$$ For all $a,b\in\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}$ and $v\in\mathcal B$, the same table gives <a id="coc:boundary-constant"></a><a id="coc:boundary-linear"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{\substack{w\in\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}\\\epsilon_w=0}}
       \sum_x p_{abw}^{x}\mu_{x,w^*}^{v}
 &=\epsilon_b\gamma_a\mu_{ab}^{v},
 \label{coc:boundary-constant}\\
 \sum_{\substack{w\in\mathcal B_{\mathfrak r}\\\epsilon_w=1}}
       \sum_x p_{abw}^{x}\mu_{x,w^*}^{v}
 &=\bigl(\gamma_b+\gamma_v+(1-\epsilon_b)\gamma_a\bigr)
       \mu_{ab}^{v}.
 \label{coc:boundary-linear}
\end{aligned}$$ There are $18^2=324$ input pairs. These two equations are the constant and linear coefficients in $H$ of <a href="#coc:boundary" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="coc:boundary">[coc:boundary]</a>: its left side gives $H^{\epsilon_w}$, while the three terms on the right give respectively $H\gamma_b\mu_{ab}^{v}$, $H\gamma_v\mu_{ab}^{v}$, and $H^{1-\epsilon_b}\gamma_a\mu_{ab}^{v}$. This verifies the identity for an indeterminate $H$, hence for either $H_1$ or $H_2$.

All these substitutions are polynomial identities in characteristic two. They require no specialization of $q$ or $H$. Finally the entries $p(t,x,J)=0$ and $p(t,y,J)=q^3f$ give $$q^2p(t,x,J)+p(t,y,J)=q^3f,$$ which is the nonzero value on the explicit cycle <a href="#coc:cycle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="coc:cycle">[coc:cycle]</a>.

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "The algebraic and homological stages of the construction. The middle row begins the stable fiber triangle; the other arrows indicate construction steps. The finite bimodule $F$ is the ordinary kernel in [branch:finite-fiber] before $Λ$ is formed. Evaluating this fiber on $X$ gives the diagonal stable map $v$; the final module is the finite quotient [conv:finite-module], using both $X$ and $v$.",
      "figure_id": "fig:construction",
      "pdf_page": 4,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:construction",
          "exact_quote": "The algebraic and homological stages of the construction.\nThe middle row begins the stable fiber triangle; the other arrows indicate\nconstruction steps. The finite bimodule $F$ is the ordinary kernel in\n\\eqref{branch:finite-fiber} before $\\",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[>=Stealth,every node/.style={font=\\small},\n box/.style={draw,rounded corners=2pt,inner sep=6pt,align=center},\n lab/.style={font=\\footnotesize,align=center}]\n\\node[box] (c) at (0,3.3) {$C$\\\\$\\dim C=10$};\n\\node[box] (t) at (4.2,3.3) {$T=C\\ltimes DC$\\\\$\\Ext_T^*(s,s)=\\kk[\\tau]$};\n\\node[box] (a) at (8.6,3.3) {$A=T\\otimes T$\\\\$X=s\\otimes s$};\n\\node[box] (f) at (0,1.4) {$F$};\n\\node[box] (u) at (4.2,1.4) {$U_{H_1}\\oplus U_{H_2}$};\n\\node[box] (cone) at (8.6,1.4) {$\\mathcal C[3]$};\n\\node[box] (lam) at (0,-.3) {$\\Lambda=\\left(\\begin{smallmatrix}A&0\\\\F&A\\end{smallmatrix}\\right)$};\n\\node[box] (z) at (4.2,-.3) {$Z$ from $X,v$};\n\\draw[->] (c)--node[above=9mm,lab]{trivial extension}(t);\n\\draw[->] (t)--node[above=9mm,lab]{tensor square}(a);\n\\draw[->] (a)--node[right,lab]{two cones\\\\and shift}(cone);\n\\draw[->] (f)--node[above,lab]{fiber projection}(u);\n\\draw[->] (u)--node[above,lab]{lifted twists}(cone);\n\\draw[->] (f)--node[left,lab]{triangular\\\\algebra}(lam);\n\\draw[->] (lam)--node[above,lab]{finite quotient}(z);\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Flowchart/commutative diagram with rectangular nodes and directed arrows arranged in three rows.\nTop row (left to right):\n- Box containing \"$C$\" and \"$\\dim C = 10$\".\n- Horizontal right-pointing arrow labeled above with \"trivial extension\".\n- Box containing \"$T = C \\ltimes DC$\" and \"$\\operatorname{Ext}^*_T(s, s) = k[\\tau]$\".\n- Horizontal right-pointing arrow labeled above with \"tensor square\".\n- Box containing \"$A = T \\otimes T$\" and \"$X = s \\otimes s$\".\n\nMiddle row:\n- Box containing \"$F$\" on the left.\n- Horizontal arrow between box \"$F$\" and box \"$U_{H_1} \\oplus U_{H_2}$\" labeled above with \"fiber projection\".\n- Box containing \"$U_{H_1} \\oplus U_{H_2}$\".\n- Horizontal right-pointing arrow labeled above with \"lifted twists\".\n- Box containing \"$\\mathcal{C}[3]$\".\n- Vertical down-pointing arrow from the top-row box \"$A = T \\otimes T$, $X = s \\otimes s$\" to the middle-row box \"$\\mathcal{C}[3]$\", labeled to the right with \"two cones\" over \"and shift\".\n\nBottom row:\n- Vertical down-pointing arrow from box \"$F$\" to box \"$\\Lambda = \\begin{pmatrix} A & 0 \\\\ F & A \\end{pmatrix}$\", labeled to the left with \"triangular\" over \"algebra\".\n- Box containing \"$\\Lambda = \\begin{pmatrix} A & 0 \\\\ F & A \\end{pmatrix}$\".\n- Horizontal right-pointing arrow labeled above with \"finite quotient\".\n- Box containing \"$Z \\text{ from } X, v$\"."
    }
  ],
